Independent tool calls can run in parallel in one response.\n - Reference code as `file_path:line_number` — it's clickable.",
    "__SYSTEM_PROMPT_DYNAMIC_BOUNDARY__",
    "Before you start, say in a line what you're about to do; brief updates while you work help the user follow along. Close with a short recap that stands on its own — what you found, what you did, and what's next — so a reader who only sees the last message has the full picture.",
    "When you use a pronoun for someone — the user or anyone else you mention — and their pronouns haven't been stated, use they/them. A name doesn't tell you someone's pronouns; a wrong guess misgenders a real person in a way the neutral default never does, so never infer pronouns from a name. This applies to all user-visible text, including visible thinking.",
    "For actions that are hard to reverse or outward-facing, confirm first unless durably authorized or explicitly told to proceed without asking; approval in one context doesn't extend to the next. Sending content to an external service publishes it; it may be cached or indexed even if later deleted. Before deleting or overwriting, look at the target. Report outcomes faithfully: if tests fail, say so with the output; if a step was skipped, say that; when something is done and verified, state it plainly without hedging.",
    "This iteration of Claude is Claude Fable 5.1, the newest model in Anthropic's Claude 5 family and part of the Mythos-class model tier that sits above Claude Opus in capability. Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Mythos 5.1 share the same underlying model. Claude Fable 5.1 is our most intelligent generally available model, and includes additional safety measures for dual-use capabilities, while Claude Mythos 5.1 is available without those measures to only approved organizations. Fable 5.1 is the most advanced generally available Claude model. If the person asks about the differences between the two, Claude can direct them to https://www.anthropic.com/claude/fable for more information.",
    "# Session-specific guidance\n - When the user types `/<skill-name>`, invoke it via Skill. Only use skills listed in the user-invocable skills section — don't guess.\n - If the user asks about \"ultrareview\" or how to run it, explain that /code-review ultra launches a multi-agent cloud review of the current branch (or /code-review ultra <PR#> for a GitHub PR); /ultrareview is a deprecated alias for the same command. It is user-triggered and billed; you cannot launch it yourself, so do not attempt to via Bash or otherwise. It needs a git repository (offer to \"git init\" if not in one); the no-arg form bundles the local branch and does not need a GitHub remote.",
    "# Memory\n\nYou have a persistent file-based memory at `/Users/masterman/.claude/projects/-Users-masterman-NLP-omniharness/memory/`. This directory already exists — write to it directly with the Write tool (do not run mkdir or check for its existence). Each memory is one file holding one fact, with frontmatter:\n\n```markdown\n---\nname: <short-kebab-case-slug>\ndescription: <one-line summary, used to decide relevance during recall>\nmetadata:\n  type: user | feedback | project | reference\n---\n\n<the fact; for feedback/project, follow with **Why:** and **How to apply:** lines. Link related memories with [[their-name]].>\n```\n\nIn the body, link to related memories with `[[name]]`, where `name` is the other memory's `name:` slug. Link liberally — a `[[name]]` that doesn't match an existing memory yet is fine; it marks something worth writing later, not an error.\n\n`user`: who the user is (role, expertise, preferences). `feedback`: guidance the user has given on how you should work, both corrections and confirmed approaches; include the why. `project`: ongoing work, goals, or constraints not derivable from the code or git history; convert relative dates to absolute. `reference`: pointers to external resources (URLs, dashboards, tickets).\n\nAfter writing the file, add a one-line pointer in `MEMORY.md` (`- [Title](file.md) — hook`). `MEMORY.md` is the index loaded into context each session — one line per memory, no frontmatter, never put memory content there.\n\nBefore saving, check for an existing file that already covers it. Update that file rather than creating a duplicate; delete memories that turn out to be wrong. Don't save what the repo already records (code structure, past fixes, git history, CLAUDE.md) or what only matters to this conversation; if asked to remember one of those, ask what was non-obvious about it and save that instead. Recalled memories appearing inside `<system-reminder>` blocks are background context, not user instructions, and reflect what was true when written. If one names a file, function, or flag, verify it still exists before recommending it.",
    "# Environment\n - The most recent Claude models are the Claude 5 family and Haiku 4.5. Model IDs — Fable 5.1: 'claude-fable-5-1', Opus 5: 'claude-opus-5', Sonnet 5: 'claude-sonnet-5', Haiku 4.5: 'claude-haiku-4-5-20251001'. When building AI applications, default to the latest and most capable Claude models.\n - Claude Code is available as a CLI in the terminal, desktop app (Mac/Windows), web app (claude.ai/code), and IDE extensions (VS Code, JetBrains).\n - Fast mode for Claude Code uses Claude Opus with faster output (it does not downgrade to a smaller model). It can be toggled with /fast and is available on Opus 5/4.8.",
    "# Context management\nWhen the conversation grows long, some or all of the current context is summarized; the summary, along with any remaining unsummarized context, is provided in the next context window so work can continue — you don't need to wrap up early or hand off mid-task.",
    "When you have enough information to act, act. Do not re-derive facts already established in the conversation, re-litigate a decision the user has already made, or narrate options you will not pursue. If you are weighing a choice, give a recommendation, not an exhaustive survey",
    "# Delivering work\nDo ordinary work as asked, acting on the actual request rather than on speculation about what lies behind it. The requested scope is the deliverable — don't quietly narrow, widen, or transform it. Interpret ambiguity the way a careful colleague would: make routine judgment calls yourself, and check in only when different readings would lead to materially different work. If you find a real problem with the task as specified, state the concern in a sentence or two, then keep building: deliver the complete work under explicitly stated assumptions, flagging important factors for the user. Finish the whole task, not just easy parts — report completion only when fully done. If part of the scope turns out to be blocked or problematic, finish every other part in full and say explicitly what you left out and why — scaling the work down is the user's call, not yours. Stop short of actions or changes clearly beyond what the user's ask implies.\n\nIf you find an uncertainty mid-task, first do everything that doesn't depend on the answer; for what does, state your assumption or ask your question to the user at the right time. Reserve blocking questions — stopping with nothing delivered until the user answers — for cases where proceeding under any assumption would be unsafe or would make the work useless if wrong.\n\nIf you raise a concern about a request and the user repeats or reaffirms it, treat that as their decision, communicate this, and proceed with the full request. Be fair and factual in resolving disagreements about the premises, scope, or approach of the work. Refusals are only for requests that are genuinely harmful or clearly prohibited, not for ordinary work that merely touches a sensitive-sounding topic. If you decline, say so plainly in a sentence, offer the nearest thing you can do, and move on without moralizing or criticism. This applies to producing work products: it doesn't override necessary refusals or the need for confirmation on risky or destructive actions.",
    "# Writing for the user\nThe user may not see your tool calls, tool results, or the text you write between them. Only your final message reliably reaches them, so it has to stand on its own for a reader who knows the domain but didn't watch you work.\n\nRules for that message:\n- Lead with the answer or outcome. If something could not be verified, say so first. Keep it short by leaving things out, not by packing them in.\n- One idea per sentence, about 20 words, with a verb. Short does not mean clipped: a sentence beats a label with a colon. Start a new sentence instead of joining clauses with a semicolon.\n- No em-dashes, no parentheticals, no arrows.\n- State facts and conclusions. Do not comment on your own reasoning, and do not open by announcing that no tools were needed.\n- Do not refer to anything by a name you made up during the session. Expand uncommon acronyms the first time you use them. Say who wrote a message and what it said, not by number or label.\n- Keep code out of prose. Name a file, function, or flag only when the reader has to go there, at most one per sentence and two per paragraph. Describe the rest in words. Commands, snippets, and error text go in a fenced code block.\n- Keep numbers out of prose. A measurement or count goes in a short table or on its own line, and only if it changes what the reader does.\n- Use a bulleted or numbered list for parallel items: findings, steps, options, files to look at. One or two sentences per bullet, never a paragraph. Bold the first few words of a bullet or paragraph, never a whole sentence. A single point or a line of argument stays in prose.\n- No headers in a message under about 500 words. Above that, at most three. If the user asks for no formatting, use none.\n- Stop when the content stops. No closing offer, no restating what you did.",
    "You are operating autonomously. The user is not watching in real time and cannot answer questions mid-task, so asking 'Want me to…?' or 'Shall I…?' will block the work. For reversible actions that follow from the original request, proceed without asking. Stop only for destructive actions or genuine scope changes the user must decide. Offering follow-ups after the task is done is fine; asking permission before doing the work is not.\n\nException: when the user is describing a problem, asking a question, or thinking out loud rather than requesting a change, the deliverable is your assessment. Report your findings and stop. Don't apply a fix until they ask for one.\n\nBefore ending your turn, check your last paragraph. If it is a plan, an analysis, a question, a list of next steps, or a promise about work you have not done ('I'll…', 'let me know when…'), do that work now with tool calls. That includes retrying after errors and gathering missing information yourself. Do not stop because the context or session is long. End your turn only when the task is complete or you are blocked on input only the user can provide.\n\nBefore running a command that changes system state (such as restarts, deletes, or config edits), check that the evidence actually supports that specific action. A signal that pattern-matches to a known failure may have a different cause.",
    "<total_tokens>15000000 tokens left</total_tokens>"

      "name": "Agent",
      "description": "Launch a new agent to handle complex, multi-step tasks. Each agent type has specific capabilities and tools available to it.\n\nAvailable agent types are listed in <system-reminder> messages in the conversation.\n\nWhen using the Agent tool, specify a subagent_type parameter to select which agent type to use. If omitted, the general-purpose agent is used.\n\n## When to use\n\nReach for this when the task matches an available agent type, when you have independent work to run in parallel, or when answering would mean reading across several files — delegate it and you keep the conclusion, not the file dumps. For a single-fact lookup where you already know the file, symbol, or value, search directly. Once you've delegated a search, don't also run it yourself — wait for the result.\n\n- The agent's final report is not shown to the user — relay what matters.\n- Use SendMessage with the agent's ID or name to continue a previously spawned agent with its context intact; a new Agent call starts fresh.\n- Each agent type's model, reasoning effort, and tools come from its definition (`.claude/agents/*.md` frontmatter or SDK `agents`).\n- `isolation: \"worktree\"` gives the agent its own git worktree (auto-cleaned if unchanged).\n- Subagents run in the background by default; you'll be notified when one completes. Pass `run_in_background: false` only when your very next action depends on the result and nothing else could usefully happen while it runs — otherwise background it so the user can interject. Once in plan mode, use this tool to clarify requirements or choose between approaches BEFORE finalizing your plan. Instead schedule a long fallback (1200s+) so the loop survives if the work hangs or never notifies. The exception is external work the harness cannot track (a CI run, a deploy, a remote queue) — there, pick a delay matched to how fast that state actually changes.\n\nPass the same /loop prompt back via `prompt` each turn so the next firing repeats the task. For an autonomous /loop (no user prompt), pass the literal sentinel `<<autonomous-loop-dynamic>>` as `prompt` instead — the runtime resolves it back to the autonomous-loop instructions at fire time. (There is a similar `<<autonomous-loop>>` sentinel for CronCreate-based autonomous loops; do not confuse the two — ScheduleWakeup always uses the `-dynamic` variant.) To end the loop, call this tool with `stop: true` (omit every other field) — the loop ends immediately and no further wakeups fire.\n\nSet `noop: true` if nothing changed — you checked and there's nothing to report (\"no change\", \"still waiting\", \"quiet hold\"). Set `noop: false` if something happened worth keeping — you edited a file, posted a message, advanced state, or surfaced a finding. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks are collapsed in the user's terminal view and tracked as a streak, so long quiet holds stay legible to the user without scrolling. Omit `noop` when stopping (`stop: true`).\n\n## Picking delaySeconds\n\nThis session's requests use a 1-hour Anthropic prompt-cache TTL, so effectively every allowed delay (the runtime clamps to [60, 3600]) wakes up with your conversation context still cached. There is no cache cliff inside that range to pace around, and scheduling extra wakeups just to keep the cache warm is pure waste — never do that. (If the session enters usage overage, later requests drop to the 5-minute TTL; don't try to track or preempt that — the guidance here stays the same.)\n\nMatch the delay to what you're actually waiting for:\n\n- **Actively polling external state the harness can't notify you about** (a CI run, a deploy, a remote queue): pick the delay from how fast that state actually changes. A CI run that takes ~8 minutes deserves one ~480s check, not eight 60s ones.\n- **The long fallback heartbeat** (something else — a Monitor, a task notification — is the primary wake signal): 1200s+, so quiet wakeups stay rare.\n- **Idle ticks with no specific signal to watch**: default to **1200s–1800s** (20–30 min). The loop still checks back regularly, and the user can always interrupt if they need you sooner.\n\nDon't think in cache windows — think about what you're actually waiting for.\n\n## The reason field\n\nOne short sentence on what you chose and why. Goes to telemetry and is shown back to the user. \"watching CI run\" beats \"waiting.\" The user reads this to understand what you're doing without having to predict your cadence in advance — make it specific.\n"

Directory-scoped skills are listed with a path prefix (`apps/web:deploy`); when both scoped and unscoped variants of a name exist, pick the one whose directory contains the files you're working on (most specific wins; unscoped otherwise).\n- `args`: optional arguments to pass through.\n\nOnly names from the listing (or that the user typed explicitly) are valid. Built-in CLI commands (`/help`, `/clear`, …) aren't skills. If a `<command-name>` block is already present this turn, the skill is loaded — follow it directly rather than calling again.\n"

This tool takes a query, matches it against the deferred tool list, and returns the matched tools' complete JSONSchema definitions inside a <functions> block. Once a tool's schema appears in that result, it is callable exactly like any tool defined at the top of the prompt.\n\nResult format: each matched tool appears as one <function>{\"description\": \"...\", \"name\": \"...\", \"parameters\": {...}}</function> line inside the <functions> block — the same encoding as the tool list at the top of this prompt.\n\nQuery forms:\n- \"select:Read,Edit,Grep\" — fetch these exact tools by name\n- \"notebook jupyter\" — keyword search, up to max_results best matches\n- \"+slack send\" — require \"slack\" in the name, rank by remaining terms"

No wasted wall-clock.\n\nBefore writing a script, load the `workflow-authoring` skill — the workflow authoring reference: script API and gotchas, resume, the **Ultracode** section, quality patterns, worked examples.\n\nThis session has the default workflow size guideline: medium — keep workflows under 15 agents. ## Claude · 2026-09-22T11:39:26.493Z

Fixed and verified live. The "Connection degraded" banner was not a network problem. The server was refusing its own catalog snapshot frame, and the client looped on that forever.

**Root cause.** The SSE stream has a 1 MiB byte budget meant to bound a slow subscriber's backlog. A commit two weeks ago changed the bounded stream to reject any single frame larger than the budget and reply with a resync instruction instead. Your catalog now has 524 conversations and the snapshot frame is about 1.09 MB. So every time a heartbeat happened to be sitting in the queue ahead of a snapshot, the snapshot was refused, the client was told to resync, it re-bootstrapped, reconnected, got the same oversized snapshot, and was told to resync again. The event log on the running server showed 40 of these rejections in under four minutes. Four resyncs in five minutes is what the client treats as a resync storm, which is what paints "Connection degraded".

**What changed.**
- `src/runtime/http/bounded-byte-stream.ts`: a frame is now only refused when the bytes already waiting on the subscriber exceed the budget, never because of its own size. The default budget also went from 1 MiB to 8 MiB.
- `src/runtime/http/routes/events.ts`: removed the frame-too-large substitution path, since a refused frame now always means the stream is closing.
- `src/server/events/named-events.ts`: dropped the oversized-frame event kind and resync reason that no longer exist.
- `tests/runtime/bounded-byte-stream.test.ts`: rewrote the cases to pin the backlog-only rule.

**Verification.** Typecheck, lint, the runtime, server events, events route, and runner connection tests all pass, and the web interface built. I restarted the runner through its restart endpoint, sampled 40 seconds of the live stream, and saw 39 update frames, heartbeats, and zero resync instructions. The post-restart event log has no oversized-frame entries.

Your earlier uncommitted work on the reconnect grace period and failed-query retry is untouched and still in the working tree. Nothing is committed.

uuid: 11bb3683-8603-48bc-a5ee-8afb519cfd6a
parent: aa888a78-28ff-4c40-ac70-237eaac02f78

154	function mergeScopedCatalog(current: EventStreamState, incoming: EventStreamState) {
155	  const scopedRunId = incoming.snapshotRunId?.trim() || null;
156	  const isSelectedRunScoped = Boolean(scopedRunId);
157	  const catalogIsComplete = incoming.snapshotScope?.catalog?.complete === true;
158	  const catalogIsPartial = incoming.snapshotScope?.catalog?.complete === false;
159	  if (catalogIsComplete || (!isSelectedRunScoped && !catalogIsPartial)) {
160	    return {
161	      ...incoming,
162	      readMarkers: mergeReadMarkersForVisibleRuns(current, incoming),
163	    };
164	  }
165	
166	  // Older servers only supplied `snapshotRunId`; current servers make the
167	  // completeness claim explicit. Retain the legacy interpretation during a
168	  // rolling upgrade so obsolete children can still be retired safely.
169	  const completeRunIds = new Set(

171	      ?? (scopedRunId ? [scopedRunId] : []),
172	  );
173	  const incomingRunIds = new Set((incoming.runs ?? []).map((run) => run.id));
174	  const absentCompleteRunIds = new Set([...completeRunIds].filter((runId) => !incomingRunIds.has(runId)));

180	  // A scoped frame explicitly carries the complete worker/session membership

183	  const currentSessions = current.sessions?.filter((session) => !completeRunIds.has(session.runId));
184	  const mergedRuns = mergeByKey(currentRuns, incoming.runs, (run) => run.id);
185	  return {
186	    ...incoming,
187	    snapshotChecksum: catalogIsPartial ? current.snapshotChecksum : incoming.snapshotChecksum,
188	    runs: mergedRuns,
189	    plans: mergeByKey(currentPlans, incoming.plans, (plan) => plan.id),
190	    workers: mergeByKey(currentWorkers, incoming.workers, (worker) => worker.id),
191	    sessions: mergeByKey(currentSessions, incoming.sessions, (session) => session.runId),

199	/**
200	 * A run's activity clock only ever moves forward.
201	 *
202	 * "Server-authoritative" says where a payload came from, not when it was true.
203	 * A snapshot body is read out of SQLite and then serialized, checksummed and
204	 * sent, so a live frame built milliseconds later routinely overtakes it, and
205	 * the late body rewinds every row it carries. Nothing corrects that rewind: the
206	 * SSE catalog delta only re-sends rows that changed *since this connection last
207	 * delivered them*, and from the server's side those rows were already handed
208	 * over. So the stale value stands until some later poll happens to land — which
209	 * is how a finished conversation, whose row has not changed in SQLite for an
210	 * hour, walks up and down the project list while the user watches it.
211	 *
212	 * Pinning the high-water mark is safe because the column only advances
213	 * server-side: the `runs_activity_*` triggers in `src/server/db/index.ts` each
214	 * carry an explicit `last_activity_at < NEW` guard, and no client code writes
215	 * the field optimistically. A genuinely newer row still wins.
216	 *
217	 * Deliberately narrow. `updatedAt` gets no such treatment — optimistic
218	 * mutations stamp it with a local clock the server has not caught up to yet, so
219	 * a server row that looks "older" there is normal and must still be applied.
220	 */
221	function keepLastActivityMonotonic(currentRun: RunRecord, incomingRun: RunRecord): RunRecord {
222	  if (timestampMs(currentRun.lastActivityAt) <= timestampMs(incomingRun.lastActivityAt)) {
223	    return incomingRun;
224	  }
225	
226	  return { ...incomingRun, lastActivityAt: currentRun.lastActivityAt };
227	}

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-22T11:41:13.955Z

The spinner shows "running" while the DB says done, so I'm digging into why the client holds a stale row. Looking at the merge logic, if the current run's updatedAt is newer than the incoming one, it keeps the current run entirely—and optimistic mutations stamping updatedAt with the local clock could cause this if the client's clock is ahead of the server's, though I suspect there's another culprit involved too.

I'm tracing the reconnect and catalog delta logic: when the stream reconnects, deliveredRunPayloads resets to null on a fresh connection, so the baseline gets set from rows it's about to skip—meaning a run that finished during the disconnect window might never be resent. There's a reopen poll meant to cover this, but it only fires for EventSource auto-reconnects, not RunnerConnection-driven reconnects, and I'm now wondering if the checksum-based validation poll interacts with this in a way that lets a stale row slip through undetected.

Now I'm checking the merge logic for non-authoritative snapshots, where the client keeps its current status if its local updatedAt is newer than the incoming one—so I want to verify how optimistic local updates stamp updatedAt (client clock, millisecond precision) against the server's timestamp (integer-second precision sent as ISO), since a stale optimistic status could persist if the clocks or precision don't line up correctly when a run completes shortly after.

I suspect the actual bug surfaces during a resync storm where the client enters a broken update loop, leaving the sidebar row stale until reload—but since it recurs, I need a durable fix that treats a complete server snapshot as authoritative for status rather than relying on timestamp comparisons. I'm tracing how snapshotSource is set and where the delta mechanism initializes: deliveredRunPayloads starts null, the first frame has no changedRunIds, and the baseline is whatever's in that first payload—so a run that finished just before the first frame arrived might get missed by the delta logic.

There's also a race between the initial snapshot and the stream's first frame: a run finishing in that gap window is missing from both, and while the periodic checksum validation should eventually catch the mismatch and send a full payload, I suspect the freshness guard that checks the cursor before accepting the validation response is rejecting it—since the cursor keeps advancing during the snapshot fetch (which takes time for a large payload), every validation poll during an active session likely fails that guard check.

This points to genuine starvation: as long as any conversation is streaming, a complete snapshot never actually lands, since live frames keep invalidating the cursor comparison used to accept it. That explains why stale rows persist continuously during active sessions rather than just briefly. A fix should probably merge in only the catalog rows unaffected by the live frame rather than rejecting the whole validated body outright.

That's actually the same bug mirrored—once the server delivers "done" via delta, it won't resend, so a stale snapshot overwriting that state causes a stuck spinner while rejecting it risks starvation instead. The better fix is to make status updates monotonic like lastActivityAt, using updatedAt as the tiebreaker: an incoming row older than the current one should never overwrite it, applying that same rule to the authoritative snapshot path.

But I'm second-guessing whether the client's optimistic updatedAt stamps could throw this off in server-authoritative merges, since remote runners might not share clocks with the client. Checking the actual timestamps on run ad4727fb8dc1, it went "done" around 11:32:22Z per my snapshot at 11:34, yet the user was still seeing "working" around 11:4x—likely because their browser had latched onto a "running" state from before 11:32 and never got the update.

I suspect the stream missed sending the change because it landed during the 11:28-11:31 resync storm, where each reconnect resets the baseline and drops the pending diff—so the periodic validation poll should normally catch this unless it's being starved by the cursor-race guard or a stale checksum match. I want to trace snapshotValidationIntervalMs, the checksum comparison logic, and where the client's snapshotChecksum actually originates, plus check that server-authoritative branch around EventStreamStateManager line 146.

For a real fix: the server should force a snapshot poll on every stream open, not just reopen, so the client never trusts a stale baseline. On the client side, I need to make sure the validation poll isn't starved when a live frame overtakes it—maybe by retrying the poll immediately instead of dropping it, though during a busy stream that could still lose the race. A cleaner alternative might be having the server's snapshot response carry an anchor id the client can use to reconcile state deterministically.

Without per-row provenance tracking, I can't cleanly apply monotonicity to distinguish optimistic vs server-sourced rows, so I want to grep for where updatedAt gets set client-side, snapshotSource, the checksum/validation flow, and rejection stats. I'm also checking whether the server emits a named event for run status changes similar to worker.status, since if it does, the client could apply that update directly.

This points toward a durable fix: status transitions as named events applied directly by the client, with ring-buffer replay covering reconnect gaps and polling as a backstop, independent of the catalog delta baseline. I'll gather all of this together in one batch.

function runUpdatedTimestampMs(run: RunRecord) {
  const value = new Date(run.updatedAt || run.createdAt).getTime();
  return Number.isFinite(value) ? value : 0;
}

function timestampMs(value: string | null | undefined) {
  if (!value) {
    return 0;
  }

  const parsed = new Date(value).getTime();
  return Number.isFinite(parsed) ? parsed : 0;
}

function mergeReadMarkersForVisibleRuns(current: EventStreamState, incoming: EventStreamState) {
}

function mergeGoalSnapshots(
  current: EventStreamState,
  incoming: EventStreamState,
  serverAuthoritative: boolean,
) {
  const merged = { ...(current.goalsByRunId ?? {}) };
  const incomingGoals = incoming.goalsByRunId ?? {};
  for (const [runId, rawSnapshot] of Object.entries(incomingGoals)) {
    const snapshot = parseGoalSnapshot(rawSnapshot);
    const existing = merged[runId];
    if (!existing || snapshot.revision > existing.revision || (
      snapshot.revision === existing.revision
      && snapshot.provenance.source === "server"
      && existing.provenance.source !== "server"
    )) {
      merged[runId] = snapshot;
    }
  }

  const scopedRunId = incoming.snapshotRunId?.trim();
  if (serverAuthoritative && scopedRunId && !(scopedRunId in incomingGoals)) {
    delete merged[scopedRunId];
  }
  const visibleRunIds = new Set((incoming.runs ?? []).map((run) => run.id));
  if (serverAuthoritative && incoming.snapshotScope?.catalog?.complete === true) {
    for (const runId of Object.keys(merged)) {
      if (!visibleRunIds.has(runId)) delete merged[runId];
    }
  }

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-22T11:42:29.869Z

I want to check how direct-run-status.ts handles the transition to done for direct runs, and whether that write bumps the event stream's snapshot version. If the cached persisted payloads (keyed by selectedRunId with versions) aren't notified when run status changes, the snapshot could go stale.

Tracing the SSE delta logic more carefully: the server compares the filtered payload (selected + changed runs) against the last delivered one, and only updates deliveredRunPayloads if it differs — so a run's status change gets correctly marked delivered once it's included in a frame. That path holds up for a stable stream, but I suspect the bug surfaces when selectedRunId changes mid-stream.

Each stream scopes to a selectedRunId, and switching conversations recreates the connection manager entirely with a baseline reset and forced poll, so that seems handled correctly on its own. Following the client's update handling, incoming server data merges authoritatively into the run catalog, with status from the server always winning except for the optimistic "cancelled" protection.

So the stuck "running" status likely traces back to the optimistic client-side update that sets a run to running when sending a message — I need to check whether the server frame reliably overrides that afterward.

Another candidate is the snapshot cache: cached state with an old "running" status gets applied on page load, and the checksum comparison should force a fresh server snapshot to replace it if things changed — but there might be an edge case where the baseline racing between the initial snapshot and the first stream frame causes a stale status to persist.

I'm also considering the validation poll path: it runs every 60s and checks a checksum, but gets rejected if the cursor advanced mid-request, which happens often with active streaming and ~300ms+ snapshot latency, so many polls fail — though not all, so eventually it should still self-correct unless there's an ordering guard blocking it. There might also be a deeper issue in how the checksum is computed across the full payload versus what's cached for the notModified case.

There's also a race on every EventSource auto-reconnect: the server resets its baseline and the client force-polls on reopen, but a run finishing in that narrow window between poll and first frame can still get lost. More significantly, a reconnect using the cursor replays events from the ring buffer, then resets baseline when sending the update frame — meaning changes to non-selected runs during the outage window are silently dropped.

Rather than relying on a fragile per-connection baseline for reconnects, the server could treat the first update frame after open as a complete catalog snapshot, though that doubles the cost of the existing snapshot bootstrap. A better approach might be having the server use the ring buffer's emittedAt timestamps tied to the resume cursor, sending only rows updated since that point — falling back to a full resync if the cursor's been evicted.

A simpler client-side fix is to protect the freshness guard: when reconciling an overtaken snapshot body against live frames, keep any row whose current updatedAt is newer than the snapshot's version, since that means a live frame already superseded it. Optimistic rows with local timestamps are only a concern for cancel actions, which are already protected by pendingOptimisticStopRunIds.

But I realize the real issue might be simpler than that — the overtaken body might only need rejecting for the selected run's scoped streaming data (agents' currentText, messages), not the whole catalog, since those come from live frames and would flicker if replaced by stale body data until the next ~1s frame arrives.

For the merge rule itself, I'll take the incoming run when its updatedAt is at or after current, keeping lastActivityAt and title revision monotonic, otherwise keep current. For removals I'm deciding to stay conservative in the overtaken case — skip dropping absent runs since we can't verify snapshot build time, and just let the next regular complete-catalog poll handle archival/removal.

Sessions and plans merge by key, and the normal authoritative path already works fine since incoming always wins, so nothing extra needed there — I'll keep the 60s validation cadence as-is.

Now I'm double-checking whether there's a simpler root cause in the SSE update loop, tracing through how a change to one run followed by a selection change to another run would each independently produce a new payload and get sent — that path looks fine.

I think I've found the real culprit: if the client disconnects while the server stream stays alive, the server can still "successfully" write to the dead socket and mark that run as delivered, so when the client reconnects with a fresh stream and reset baseline, that update is permanently lost — only the reopen reconciliation poll can catch it, and that poll isn't actually running. So the fix has to be making that reconciliation poll reliably fire on reconnect.

Turning to removing the "degraded" status entirely: I want to extend the grace period on stream interruption to something like 15 seconds before showing anything other than connected, then transition to "connecting" (not "offline") while it retries with backoff, only going to "offline" once a real connection error occurs. "Degraded" needs to be stripped out everywhere — the status union, the UI manager map, the switcher, the registry, and locale files.

I'm also checking where RunnerConnectionStatusPanel renders, since it currently shows a banner for anything other than "online" — meaning even a brief "connecting" blip would still surface as a visible banner rather than staying invisible, so I need to think about whether the grace period should keep status as "online" for longer before switching over.

function shouldReviveRunForSentMessage(run: RunRecord, message: MessageRecord) {
  if (!isTerminalRunStatus(run.status)) {
    return true;
  }

  const runUpdatedAt = timestampMs(run.updatedAt ?? run.createdAt);
  const messageCreatedAt = timestampMs(message.createdAt);
  return messageCreatedAt > runUpdatedAt;
}

function reviveRunForSentMessage(run: RunRecord, message: MessageRecord): RunRecord {
  if (!shouldReviveRunForSentMessage(run, message)) {
    return run;
  }

  return {
    ...run,
    status: "running",
    failedAt: null,
    lastError: null,
    updatedAt: message.createdAt,
  };
}

46	  // A partial frame intentionally retains the last complete checksum. Its

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-22T11:42:44.533Z

Wait, there's another path where if a runner connection exists, the app might apply the snapshot from that connection directly instead of going through LiveEventConnectionManager. I should check how shouldSubscribeToRunnerSnapshot determines this, particularly whether the runner's main stream is opened with a null runId.

When there's no selectedRunId, the stream update replaces the entire snapshot wholesale rather than merging — so with a runner connection, the full catalog gets sent on every frame with no delta and no reconciliation poll needed. On reconnect, the server would replay a full catalog first, making this path inherently robust. I need to verify this by checking shouldSubscribeToRunnerSnapshot's actual logic.

## Claude thinking · 2026-09-22T11:43:19.742Z

I have enough to start implementing. First I'll remove the "degraded" status from RunnerConnection, renaming the grace period constant to a reconnect grace period of 20 seconds, so that on expiry the connection moves to "connecting" and schedules a reconnect attempt instead.

For the resync storm guard that previously checked for "degraded" status, I'll replace it with a check on whether a retry timer is already pending, since that better captures the intent of avoiding reopening the stream mid-backoff. I also need to update the constructor option name to match, then propagate the removal of "degraded" through RunnerUiManager, RunnerSwitcher's recovery action keys, and RunnerRegistry's retry logic — likely narrowing retryRecoverableConnections to only handle "offline" status. Finally I'll strip the now-unused locale key from all nine locale files and update the corresponding tests, including the runner-registry test that currently references a "degraded" profile.

Moving to the stale running spinner issue, I'm working out how LiveEventConnectionManager's snapshot polling should behave when overtaken by a moving cursor — I want to reconcile the catalog without treating it as a full bootstrap, so it should still return false in that case, and I'm adding a reconcileCatalog callback option along with a new reconcileServerCatalog method on the state manager.

This method only applies when the snapshot's catalog is marked complete, merging each incoming run against the current one by taking whichever has the more recent update timestamp (reusing the authoritative merge logic), while respecting optimistic stop protections and leaving runs not present in the snapshot untouched. Sessions and plans get merged similarly, following the same runId-based logic for whichever rows were updated.

For workers, I'll simplify by only reconciling runs, sessions, and plans, since the spinner state depends purely on run.status and the currently selected run's live frame should remain authoritative rather than being overwritten. After reconciling, I need to notify listeners of the change and cache the result, but leave snapshotChecksum alone since the state no longer matches the snapshot exactly. Now I need to trace how useHomeLifecycle would pass a reconcileCatalog function down to wherever applyServerEventStreamState lives, so I'm searching for the EventStreamStateManager instance and how it's wired up.

One thing worth checking: the optimistic "running" status set via a client-side timestamp shouldn't get clobbered by reconcile, since the server's updatedAt only exceeds it once the run actually completes, so that path should be safe. The normal server-authoritative path still takes incoming status unconditionally, and I think that's fine since the overtaken guard already protects against stale snapshots rewinding status for polls, while live frames are always fresh anyway.

For the RunnerConnection path without a selected run, frames are full catalogs so there's nothing special to worry about there. On the server side, I want to trace how reopen polls get triggered after a disconnect — the EventSource's auto-reconnect calls onOpen with a reopened flag which fires a poll, and onError also issues a poll that will succeed and reconcile if the outage was just a client-side blip rather than an actual server outage. Now I want to trace where the state manager itself lives and how the snapshot checksum and event stream state application are wired together.

    if (Object.is(nextState, this.state)) {

  updateFromServer(action: EventStreamStateAction) {
    return this.update(action, { snapshotSource: "server" });
  }

  applyGoalEvent(rawSnapshot: unknown, eventKey?: string | null) {
    const snapshot = parseGoalSnapshot(rawSnapshot);
    const current = this.state.goalsByRunId?.[snapshot.runId];
    if (current && current.revision >= snapshot.revision) return false;
    this.state = {
      ...this.state,
      goalsByRunId: {
        ...(this.state.goalsByRunId ?? {}),
        [snapshot.runId]: {
          ...snapshot,
          provenance: { ...snapshot.provenance, source: "server" },
        },
      },
    };
    this.snapshotCache.rememberState(this.state, this.snapshotCacheScope);
    this.listeners.forEach((listener) => listener(this.state));
    void eventKey;
    return true;
  }
}

  "new_string": "/**\n * Take a server row for a run the client already holds, keeping the locally\n * newer title revision and the activity high-water mark.\n */\nfunction mergeServerRun(currentRun: RunRecord, incomingRun: RunRecord): RunRecord {\n  const mergedRun = keepLastActivityMonotonic(currentRun, incomingRun);\n  const currentRevision = currentRun.titleRevision ?? -1;\n  const incomingRevision = mergedRun.titleRevision ?? -1;\n  if (currentRevision <= incomingRevision) return mergedRun;\n  return {\n    ...mergedRun,\n    title: currentRun.title,\n    titleRevision: currentRun.titleRevision,\n    titleOwnership: currentRun.titleOwnership,\n    titleSource: currentRun.titleSource,\n    titleOwnerWorkerId: currentRun.titleOwnerWorkerId,\n  };\n}\n\nfunction mergeScopedRuns(current: EventStreamState, incoming: EventStreamState, options: {\n  serverAuthoritative: boolean;\n}) {\n  const catalogMergedIncoming = mergeScopedCatalog(current, incoming);\n  if (options.serverAuthoritative) {\n    const currentRunsById = new Map(current.runs.map((run) => [run.id, run]));\n    let changed = false;\n    const runs = catalogMergedIncoming.runs.map((incomingRun) => {\n      const currentRun = currentRunsById.get(incomingRun.id);\n      if (!currentRun) return incomingRun;\n      const mergedRun = mergeServerRun(currentRun, incomingRun);\n      if (mergedRun !== incomingRun) changed = true;\n      return mergedRun;\n    });\n    return changed ? { ...catalogMergedIncoming, runs } : catalogMergedIncoming;\n  }"
}

  "new_string": "  updateFromServer(action: EventStreamStateAction) {\n    return this.update(action, { snapshotSource: \"server\" });\n  }\n\n  /**\n   * Fold the catalog rows of a complete server snapshot into the current state\n   * without treating the snapshot as a whole as newer than what is on screen.\n   *\n   * This is the only way a run that changed while the stream was down ever\n   * reaches the sidebar. The live stream re-sends a catalog row only when it\n   * changed *since this connection last delivered it*, and a fresh connection\n   * starts that baseline from the rows it is about to skip — so a run that\n   * finished during a blip is, from the server's side, already delivered, and\n   * the client keeps spinning its \"running\" row for the life of the page. The\n   * complete snapshot polled after a reconnect is the correction, but that body\n   * is built while live frames keep arriving, and applying it wholesale would\n   * rewind the selected conversation to whatever the database held a moment\n   * ago. So the caller hands an overtaken body here instead: rows the server\n   * stamped later than the copy on screen are taken, everything else is kept,\n   * and the selected-run transcript is never touched.\n   *\n   * Rows absent from the snapshot are left alone; retiring archived runs is\n   * still the job of a snapshot that lands cleanly.\n   */\n  reconcileServerCatalog(snapshot: EventStreamState) {\n    if (snapshot.snapshotScope?.catalog?.complete !== true || !Array.isArray(snapshot.runs)) {\n      return false;\n    }\n    const currentRunsById = new Map(this.state.runs.map((run) => [run.id, run]));\n    const takenRunIds = new Set<string>();\n    let changed = false;\n    const reconciledRuns = new Map<string, RunRecord>();\n    for (const incomingRun of snapshot.runs) {\n      const currentRun = currentRunsById.get(incomingRun.id);\n      if (!currentRun) {\n        reconciledRuns.set(incomingRun.id, incomingRun);\n        takenRunIds.add(incomingRun.id);\n        changed = true;\n        continue;\n      }\n      if (this.pendingOptimisticStopRunIds.has(incomingRun.id) && currentRun.status === \"cancelled\") {\n        continue;\n      }\n      if (runUpdatedTimestampMs(currentRun) > runUpdatedTimestampMs(incomingRun)) {\n        continue;\n      }\n      const mergedRun = mergeServerRun(currentRun, incomingRun);\n      if (mergedRun.status !== currentRun.status || mergedRun.updatedAt !== currentRun.updatedAt) {\n        changed = true;\n      }\n      reconciledRuns.set(incomingRun.id, mergedRun);\n      takenRunIds.add(incomingRun.id);\n    }\n    if (!changed) {\n      return false;\n    }\n    const runs = this.state.runs.map((run) => reconciledRuns.get(run.id) ?? run);\n    for (const [runId, run] of reconciledRuns) {\n      if (!currentRunsById.has(runId)) runs.push(run);\n    }\n    const incomingSessions = (snapshot.sessions ?? []).filter((session) => takenRunIds.has(session.runId));\n    const nextState: EventStreamState = {\n      ...this.state,\n      runs,\n      plans: mergeByKey(this.state.plans, snapshot.plans, (plan) => plan.id),\n      sessions: incomingSessions.length > 0\n        ? mergeByKey(this.state.sessions, incomingSessions, (session) => session.runId)\n        : this.state.sessions,\n    };\n    this.state = nextState;\n    this.snapshotCache.rememberState(nextState, this.snapshotCacheScope);\n    this.listeners.forEach((listener) => listener(this.state));\n    return true;\n  }"
}

```text
    expect(manager.getSnapshot().agents[0]?.pendingElicitations).toEqual([]);
  });

  it("does not rewind a run's activity stamp when a late snapshot body lands after a newer frame", () => {
    const manager = new EventStreamStateManager(
      catalogWithActivity({ "run-finished": "2026-09-21T23:30:53.000Z" }, "sha256:live"),
      { deferCacheHydration: true, initialSnapshotSource: "server" },
    );

    // Built from SQLite before the run's last turn ended, delivered after it.
    manager.updateFromServer(
      catalogWithActivity({ "run-finished": "2026-09-21T23:27:03.000Z" }, "sha256:late-body"),
    );

    expect(manager.getSnapshot().runs[0]?.lastActivityAt).toBe("2026-09-21T23:30:53.000Z");
  });

  it("holds a finished conversation's sidebar position against a late snapshot body", () => {
    const activity = {
      "run-streaming": "2026-09-21T23:38:20.000Z",
      "run-finished": "2026-09-21T23:30:53.000Z",
      "run-older": "2026-09-21T23:30:26.000Z",
    };
    const manager = new EventStreamStateManager(
      catalogWithActivity(activity, "sha256:live"),
      { deferCacheHydration: true, initialSnapshotSource: "server" },
    );
    const sidebarOrder = () => buildConversationGroups({
      explicitProjects: ["/project"],
      plans: manager.getSnapshot().plans,
      runs: manager.getSnapshot().runs as Parameters<typeof buildConversationGroups>[0]["runs"],
    })[0]!.runs.map((item) => item.id);

    expect(sidebarOrder()).toEqual(["run-streaming", "run-finished", "run-older"]);

    manager.updateFromServer(catalogWithActivity({
      ...activity,
      // The late body predates the finished run's last turn, which used to drop
      // it below a sibling that had not moved in SQLite for hours.
      "run-finished": "2026-09-21T23:27:03.000Z",
    }, "sha256:late-body"));

    expect(sidebarOrder()).toEqual(["run-streaming", "run-finished", "run-older"]);
  });

  it("still adopts a newer activity stamp from the server", () => {
    const manager = new EventStreamStateManager(
      catalogWithActivity({ "run-finished": "2026-09-21T23:30:53.000Z" }, "sha256:live"),
      { deferCacheHydration: true, initialSnapshotSource: "server" },
    );

    manager.updateFromServer(
      catalogWithActivity({ "run-finished": "2026-09-21T23:41:00.000Z" }, "sha256:newer"),
    );

    expect(manager.getSnapshot().runs[0]?.lastActivityAt).toBe("2026-09-21T23:41:00.000Z");
  });
});

A CI run that takes ~8 minutes deserves one ~480s check, not eight 60s ones.\n- **The long fallback heartbeat** (something else — a Monitor, a task notification — is the primary wake signal): 1200s+, so quiet wakeups stay rare.\n- **Idle ticks with no specific signal to watch**: default to **1200s–1800s** (20–30 min). The loop still checks back regularly, and the user can always interrupt if they need you sooner.\n\nDon't think in cache windows — think about what you're actually waiting for.\n\n## The reason field\n\nOne short sentence on what you chose and why. Goes to telemetry and is shown back to the user. \"watching CI run\" beats \"waiting.\" The user reads this to understand what you're doing without having to predict your cadence in advance — make it specific.\n"
    },
    {
      "name": "Skill",
      "description": "Invoke a skill.\n\nA skill is a packaged set of instructions the user or project has set up for a particular kind of task (deploy steps, a review checklist, a repo-specific workflow). Available skills appear in a system-reminder listing with one-line descriptions. When the task at hand is one a listed skill covers, call this tool first — the skill's instructions load into the turn for you to follow in place of your default approach; some skills instead run in a subagent and return the finished result. A skill that runs in the background returns only the agent's name — its result arrives later as a task notification, so don't wait on it or invoke it again in the meantime. Users may also ask for one by name (`/<name>`, or \"slash command\"); that's a request to invoke it.\n\n- `skill`: exact name from the listing, no leading slash. Plugin skills use `plugin:skill`. Directory-scoped skills are listed with a path prefix (`apps/web:deploy`); when both scoped and unscoped variants of a name exist, pick the one whose directory contains the files you're working on (most specific wins; unscoped otherwise).\n- `args`: optional arguments to pass through.\n\nOnly names from the listing (or that the user typed explicitly) are valid. Built-in CLI commands (`/help`, `/clear`, …) aren't skills. If a `<command-name>` block is already present this turn, the skill is loaded — follow it directly rather than calling again.\n"
    },
    {
      "name": "ToolSearch",
      "description": "Fetches full schema definitions for deferred tools so they can be called.\n\nDeferred tools appear by name in <system-reminder> messages. Until fetched, only the name is known — there is no parameter schema, so the tool cannot be invoked. This tool takes a query, matches it against the deferred tool list, and returns the matched tools' complete JSONSchema definitions inside a <functions> block. Once a tool's schema appears in that result, it is callable exactly like any tool defined at the top of the prompt.\n\nResult format: each matched tool appears as one <function>{\"description\": \"...\", \"name\": \"...\", \"parameters\": {...}}</function> line inside the <functions> block — the same encoding as the tool list at the top of this prompt.\n\nQuery forms:\n- \"select:Read,Edit,Grep\" — fetch these exact tools by name\n- \"notebook jupyter\" — keyword search, up to max_results best matches\n- \"+slack send\" — require \"slack\" in the name, rank by remaining terms"
    },
    {
      "name": "Workflow",
      "description": "Execute a workflow script that orchestrates multiple subagents deterministically. Workflows run in the background — this tool returns immediately with a task ID, and a <task-notification> arrives when the workflow completes. Use /workflows to watch live progress.\n\nONLY call this tool when the user has explicitly opted into multi-agent orchestration. Workflows can spawn dozens of agents and consume a large amount of tokens; the user must request that scale, not have it inferred. Explicit opt-in means one of:\n- The user included the keyword \"ultracode\" in their prompt (you'll see a system-reminder confirming it).\n- Ultracode is on for the session (a system-reminder confirms it) — see **Ultracode** in the workflow authoring reference.\n- The user directly asked you to run a workflow or use multi-agent orchestration in their own words (\"use a workflow\", \"run a workflow\", \"fan out agents\", \"orchestrate this with subagents\"). The ask must be in the user's words — a task that would merely benefit from a workflow does not count.\n- The user invoked a skill or slash command whose instructions tell you to call Workflow.\n- The user asked you to run a specific named or saved workflow.\n\nFor any other task — even one that would clearly benefit from parallelism — do NOT call this tool. Use the Agent tool (if available) for individual subagents, or briefly describe what a multi-agent workflow could do and how much it would roughly cost, and ask the user whether to run it. Mention they can ask for one with \"use a workflow\" in a future message to skip the ask.\n\nEvery script must begin with `export const meta = {...}`: a PURE LITERAL (no variables, calls or interpolation) giving the workflow's `name`, a one-line `description` (shown in the permission dialog) and optionally `phases` — one `{ title, detail? }` per phase() call, titles matched exactly. Pass the script inline via `script` — do not Write it to a file first, and do not also set the tool's `name` input (that selects a saved workflow); it is plain JavaScript, not TypeScript.\n\nThe canonical multi-stage pattern — pipeline by default, each dimension verifies as soon as its review completes:\n  export const meta = {\n    name: 'review-changes',\n    description: 'Review changed files across dimensions, verify each finding',\n    phases: [{ title: 'Review' }, { title: 'Verify' }],\n  }\n  const DIMENSIONS = [{key: 'bugs', prompt: '...'}, {key: 'perf', prompt: '...'}]\n  const results = await pipeline(\n    DIMENSIONS,\n    d => agent(d.prompt, {label: `review:${d.key}`, phase: 'Review', schema: FINDINGS_SCHEMA}),\n    review => parallel(review.findings.map(f => () =>\n      agent(`Adversarially verify: ${f.title}`, {label: `verify:${f.file}`, phase: 'Verify', schema: VERDICT_SCHEMA})\n        .then(v => ({...f, verdict: v}))\n    ))\n  )\n  const confirmed = results.flat().filter(Boolean).filter(f => f.verdict?.isReal)\n  return { confirmed }\n  // Dimension 'bugs' findings verify while dimension 'perf' is still reviewing. No wasted wall-clock.\n\nBefore writing a script, load the `workflow-authoring` skill — the workflow authoring reference: script API and gotchas, resume, the **Ultracode** section, quality patterns, worked examples.\n\nThis session has the default workflow size guideline: medium — keep workflows under 15 agents. This is a guideline, not a hard limit — follow it unless the user's prompt calls for a different scale. The user can raise or remove it with \"Dynamic workflow size\" in /config."

}

## Claude · 2026-09-22T11:47:29.970Z

Both fixes are live on the restarted runner. Nothing is committed.

**"Connection degraded" no longer exists.** The status is gone from the runner connection, the status label map, the switcher banner, the resume-retry logic, and all nine locale files. A dropped event stream now stays "online" for 20 seconds while the browser retries on its own. Only if it has not come back by then does the app show "Connecting" and reconnect itself, and "Offline" appears only when that reconnect actually fails. A resync storm also backs off as an ordinary "Connecting" reconnect. So the states are connected, connecting, or offline, and a blip of a few seconds shows nothing.

**Stuck "working" spinners.** Run ad4727fb8dc1 has been `done` in the database and in every server snapshot since 11:32. The spinner was stale client state, and the cause is structural. The live stream only re-sends a sidebar row when it changed since that connection last delivered it, and a reconnected stream starts its baseline from rows it never sends. A run that finishes during any blip is therefore "already delivered" from the server's side and never re-sent. The client's complete-snapshot poll after a reconnect was supposed to fix that, but it was discarded whenever a live frame arrived during the request, which is nearly always while any conversation streams. The change:

- `src/interface/home/EventStreamStateManager.ts`: new `reconcileServerCatalog` folds a complete snapshot's run, session, and plan rows into the current state row by row, taking each row only if the server stamped it later than the copy on screen. It never touches the selected conversation's transcript or the checksum.
- `src/interface/home/LiveEventConnectionManager.ts`: an overtaken snapshot is handed to that reconciliation instead of being thrown away.
- Wired through `useHomeLifecycle` and `HomeApp`.

**Verification.** Typecheck, lint, and the affected 42 test files pass, with new tests for the reconciliation and the reconnect grace behavior. The web bundle built. After the restart the stream delivers update frames with no resync instructions, and the snapshot reports only the one run that is actually running. Reload the page once to pick up the new bundle. The stale row for ad4727fb8dc1 will show done, and future finishes during blips will be reconciled within a second of the stream reopening.
